# 2604.14346v2_Fluctuations_for_the_Toda_lattice

## Page 1


FLUCTUATIONS FOR THE TODA LATTICE
AMOL AGGARWAL AND MATTHEW NICOLETTI
Abstract. In this paper we consider the Toda lattice (p(t); q(t)) at thermal equilibrium, meaning
that its variables (pj) and (eqj−qj+1) are independent Gaussian and Gamma random variables,
respectively. We show under diffusive scaling that the space-time fluctuations for the model’s
currents converge to an explicit Gaussian limit. As consequences, we deduce, (i) the scaling limit
for the trajectory of a single particle q0 is a Brownian motion; (ii) space-time two-point correlation
functions for the model decay inversely with time, with explicit scaling distributions predicted by
Doyon [24] and Spohn [73]. Our starting point is the notion that the Toda lattice can be thought
of as a dense collection of many “quasi-particles” that interact through scattering. The core of
our work is to establish that the full joint scaling limit of the fluctuations for these quasi-particles
is given by a Gaussian process, called a dressed L´evy–Chentsov field.

1. Introduction
1.1. Overview. A central question in mathematical physics is to describe the long-time behav-
ior of many-body Hamiltonian systems.
An important aspect of this is to determine universal
scaling limits for their space-time fluctuations at equilibrium. Much has now been established in
1
arXiv:2604.14346v2  [math.PR]  18 May 2026


## Page 2


2
AMOL AGGARWAL AND MATTHEW NICOLETTI
this direction if the system’s density of particles is vanishingly low; see the review of Bodineau–
Gallagher–Saint-Raymond–Simonella [9]. However, little is known if this density is strictly positive,
even in one spatial dimension.
The broad belief in this context is that the limiting fluctuations for these systems should separate
into universality classes, depending on whether the underlying dynamics are chaotic or integrable. In
the chaotic case, physics works of van Beijeren [81] and Spohn [72] posit Kardar–Parisi–Zhang (KPZ)
[46] type fluctuations; after a long time T ≫1, certain space-time two-point correlation functions
should decay as T −2/3 along particular characteristic directions, and the system’s fluctuations
should be of order T 1/3 and scale to non-Gaussian limits. For various stochastic interacting particle
systems, this (and substantially more) is now established; see the surveys of Corwin [16] and Quastel
[65]. However, proving such phenomena for any deterministic Hamiltonian system remains open.
In the integrable case, physics works of De Nardis–Bernard–Doyon [19, 20] and Gopalakrishnan–
Huse–Khemani–Vasseur [40] instead predict that diffusive fluctuations typically arise; after a long
time T ≫1, space-time two-point correlation functions should decay as T −1 along all directions,
and the system’s fluctuations should be of order T 1/2 and scale to a non-trivially correlated Gauss-
ian process. The only model for which such phenomena has previously been proven (as stochastic
dynamics no longer serve as suitable proxies) is the hard rods model; see the earlier works of
Boldrighini–Dobrushin–Suhov [12, 13], Spohn [71], Presutti–Wick [64], and the more recent devel-
opments by Ferrari–Franceschini–Grevino–Spohn [35] and Ferrari–Olla [37].
While the hard rods model provided a useful initial test case, it has simplifying features (such as
conservation of each individual particle’s momentum, and a constant scattering shift) that raise the
question of whether its fluctuation behavior fully captures that of Hamiltonian integrable systems
more broadly. This warrants understanding the above predictions for a more representative such
system with richer interactions. The purpose of this paper is to do so for the Toda lattice.
The Toda lattice [79] is a Hamiltonian system (p(t); q(t)), where p(t) = (pi(t)) and q(t) = (qi(t))
are indexed by i ∈Z, evolving under the equations
∂tqi(t) = pi(t),
and
∂tpi(t) = eqi−1(t)−qi(t) −eqi(t)−qi+1(t).
As these dynamics are invariant under a simultaneous shift of all (qi), we normalize q0(0) = 0. This
model may be thought of as a system of particles moving on the real line, with locations (qi) and
momenta (pi). Since the works of Flaschka [39] and Manakov [52], which exhibited its full set of
conserved quantities, and that of Moser [59], which determined its scattering shift, the Toda lattice
has become recognized as an archetypal example of an integrable system.
The long-time behavior of the Toda lattice has been extensively studied when its initial data
either decays or approximates an almost everywhere smooth profile (so that its solutions have a well-
defined background). In these cases, detailed results have been attained (often by analyzing asso-
ciated Riemann–Hilbert problems) by Venakides–Deift–Oba [82], Deift–Kamvissis–Kriecherbauer–
Zhou [21], Deift–McLaughlin [22], Bloch–Golse–Paul–Uribe [7], and Kr¨uger–Teschl [48].
In this work, we study the Toda lattice under equilibrium initial conditions, which are necessarily
rough and fully supported on Z. Specifically, we consider the model under perhaps its most natural
invariant measure,1 sometimes called thermal equilibrium, which is when the (pj) and (e(qj−qj+1)/2)
are independent Gaussian and chi random variables, respectively. Fixing two parameters β, θ > 0,
this means that we sample each pj and e(qj−qj+1)/2 independently, under the densities Cβe−βx2/2
and Cβ,θx2θ−1e−βx2/2 · 1x>0, respectively (where Cβ and Cβ,θ are normalization constants).
1This is a random initial condition (p(0); q(0)), so that the law of (p(t); q(t)) equals that of (p(0); q(0)) for all t.

Now we state several sample results. To lighten the notation in this introductory section, these
results may be written informally (often without giving full definitions or equations) or in a special
case. However, we will provide specific references to Section 2 for the precise statements in full
generality. Throughout, we will assume that θ is sufficiently small with respect to β.2
Our first result identifies the scaling limit for the trajectory of a single particle, say q0, in the
Toda lattice, as a Brownian motion.
Theorem A (Corollary 2.13). The process T −1/2 · q0(Tτ) converges to a Brownian motion in τ
with an explicit variance (given by (2.22) below), as T tends to ∞.
Next, it is natural to ask for the asymptotics of two-point correlation functions after a long time
T ≫1. Indeed, these were initially sought after in the physics literature soon after the original
discovery of the Toda lattice [70, 23, 69], but a conjectural answer did not emerge at the time.
More recently, Doyon [24] and then Spohn [73] predicted that these correlations decay as T −1
with an explicit scaling function, which was later tested numerically to high accuracy by Mazzuca–
Grava–Kriecherbauer–McLaughlin–Mendl–Spohn [56]. Corollary 2.15 below verifies this prediction
in generality (for arbitrary local charges). Here we only informally state a special case of this result,
addressing the two-point correlation function E[p0(0)p⌊xT ⌋(T)] between particle momenta.
Theorem B (Corollary 2.15, special case). The two-point correlation function T ·E[p0(0)p⌊xT ⌋(T)]
converges, as a distribution in x, to an explicit scaling function (given by the (m, n) = (1, 1) case
of (2.27) below), as T tends to ∞.
Theorems A and B will be consequences of a broader space-time fluctuation scaling limit result
for currents of the Toda lattice. Theorem 2.12 below provides the general form of this result (for
currents of arbitrary local charges); here we only informally state a special case of it, addressing
the particle current. Below, given real numbers t ≥0 and x, y ∈R, let Jt(x, y) denote the flux of
particles across the interval [x, y] after time t, namely, Jt(x, y) = #{j : qj(0) < x, qj(t) ≥y}−#{j :
qj(0) ≥x, qj(t) < y}.
Theorem C (Theorem 2.12, special case). The process T −1/2 · (JT τ(Tq, Tq′) −E[JT τ(Tq, Tq′)])
converges to an explicit Gaussian limit in (τ, q, q′) (given by (2.20) below), as T tends to ∞.
We will deduce Theorem C (and thus Theorems A and B) from a “quasi-particle” fluctuation
result, given by Theorem D below. Before setting up the latter, let us comment on how the above
three theorems relate to the literature on kinetic theory and stochastic dynamics.
First, for the hard spheres model under the low-density, Boltzmann–Grad limit, the direct coun-
terparts (witnessing Gaussian limits under similar scaling) of Theorems A, B, and C were estab-
lished by Bodineau–Gallagher–Saint-Raymond–Simonella in [8], [10], and [11], respectively. For the
weakly nonlinear Schr¨odinger equation, the counterpart of Theorem B was proven by Lukkarinen–
Spohn in [51] (where the question of understanding two-point correlation functions for strongly
nonlinear integrable systems, such as the Toda lattice, was incidentally also mentioned).
At positive density and strong interaction, the above results had only been shown before for the
hard rods model; the analogs of Theorems B and C were proven in [71] and [64, 13, 37], respectively.
Partial results have also been obtained for the box-ball model, an integrable cellular automaton. For
2This corresponds to assuming that density of particles in the system is not too large (but is still strictly positive).
Its main source is our use of certain estimates from [2], where this assumption was imposed to verify that a specific
matrix was (quantitatively) invertible; see [2, Remark 6.7]. We expect this invertibility to hold for all θ, so it is likely
that this condition is artificial. However, we will make no effort to remove it here.

this system, Croydon–Kato–Sasada–Tsujimoto [17] showed tightness of a particle’s trajectory under
diffusive scaling (in the direction of Theorem A), and identified the particle current fluctuations at
a specific space-time point (a marginal for the counterpart of Theorem C at this point).
For various stochastic interacting particle systems, such as the totally asymmetric simple ex-
clusion process (TASEP), much is now known. After the initial current fluctuation results for the
TASEP by Johansson [44], Ferrari, Pr¨ahofer, and Spohn [63, 38] showed that its two-point function
(as in Theorem B) decay as T −2/3 along a characteristic line, and limit to a universal scaling func-
tion. Matetski–Quastel–Remenik [54] later showed that its full space-time current fluctuations (as
in Theorem C) are of order T 1/3 and rescale to a non-Gaussian process called the KPZ fixed point.
Theorems A, B, and C give the fluctuation scaling limit for the Toda lattice and show it differs
(both in scaling exponents and limiting distributions) from that of stochastic dynamics such as the
TASEP, in agreement with the predictions described earlier. This is the first such result for a dense,
strongly interacting, deterministic Hamiltonian system beyond the hard rods model.
1.2. Quasi-particles. The starting point for our analysis is the notion that the Toda lattice can
be viewed as a dense collection of objects called quasi-particles, each possessing a time-independent
spectral parameter λj and a time-dependent location Qj(t). To define them, it is convenient3 to
truncate the index j ∈Z, of the Toda lattice variables (pj; qj), to a long interval [N1, N2].
As the quasi-particle spectral parameters are time-independent, they are given by the conserved
quantities of the Toda lattice, which are the eigenvalues (λ1, λ2, . . . , λN) of its Lax matrix. This
is the tridiagonal, symmetric matrix L(t) = [Lij(t)], whose diagonal and off-diagonal entries are
the (pi(t)) and (e(qi(t)−qi+1(t))/2), respectively. When N = N2 −N1 + 1 is large and (p(t); q(t)) is
random, L(t) becomes a high-dimensional random matrix, whose eigenvalue density prescribes the
distribution of quasi-particle spectral parameters in the Toda lattice under thermal equilibrium.
After initial work of Opper [62], Spohn [74] predicted formulas for this limiting density (under
a broader class of invariant measures), by relating random Lax matrices to high temperature β
ensembles. These predictions were proven in works of Grava, Guionnet, Kozlowski, Little, Mazzuca,
and Memin [55, 43, 57, 42], with [43, 42] also providing large deviations principles for these densities.
A definition for the quasi-particle positions (Qj(t)) was provided in [1], in terms of the eigenvec-
tors of L(t). To explain it (simplifying slightly; see Definition 2.18 below for its full description),
let uj(t) = (uj(i; t))i∈[N1,N2] denote a unit eigenvector of L(t) with eigenvalue λj. Results on ran-
dom tridiagonal matrices due to Kunz–Souillard [49] and Schenker [68] imply that, if L(t) is under
thermal equilibrium, then uj(t) is exponentially localized. This means that it admits some “center”
φt(j) ∈[N1, N2] such that |uj(i; t)| ≤Ce−c|i−φt(j)| likely holds for any i ∈[N1, N2]. We view this
center φt(j) as the index of the Toda particle associated with the j-th quasi-particle. So, we define
the j-th quasi-particle’s location on R to be this particle’s position Qj(t) = qφt(j)(t).
In [1], it was proven that the above quasi-particle data (λj, Qj(t)) has two properties that could
potentially be used together to study asymptotics for the Toda lattice. First, natural quantities
describing the original Toda lattice, such as currents, are closely approximable by simple expressions
of the quasi-particles (for example, the number of Toda particles in an interval is equal to the number
of quasi-particles in it). Second, these quasi-particles satisfy the approximate equation
Qk(t) = Qk(0) + λkt + 2

FLUCTUATIONS FOR THE TODA LATTICE

In the physics literature, the concept of viewing many-body integrable systems as dense col-
lections of quasi-particles was posited (though without providing a microscopic definition of their
locations (Qj)) by Bertini–Collura–De Nardis–Fagotti [5] and Castro-Alvaredo–Doyon–Yoshimura
[14], and variants of (1.1) were widely predicted by Doyon–H¨ubner–Yoshimura [29, 27, 28]. One
may informally interpret (1.1) as follows. The k-th quasi-particle Qk moves with velocity λk until
it meets another one Qj. At that moment, Qk instantaneously moves forward or backward by
2 log |λk −λj|, depending on whether it met Qj from the right or left, respectively (this could make
it pass another quasi-particle, producing a “cascade” of such interactions). Then, Qk proceeds at
velocity λk until meeting another quasi-particle, when the procedure repeats. Here, 2 log |λk −λj|
is the Toda lattice’s scattering shift, describing the phase displacement of just two quasi-particles
passing through each other.
As observed by Zakharov [84] and El–Kamchatnov–Pavlov–Zykov
[33, 34], this description parallels the collision structure between solitons in classical integrable sys-
tems. Indeed, solitons also proceed independently until they get close, after which they undergo an
intricate interaction, and then emerge as independent solitons. Their shapes (spectral parameters)
are the same as before the interaction, but their locations are translated (relative to their free
evolutions) by a scattering shift. Thus, we refer to (1.1) as the asymptotic scattering relation.4
Given the definition of the (Qj) and that they asymptotically determine the Toda lattice currents,
the next question is to understand the limiting behavior of these quasi-particles. In [2], it was proven
by analyzing (1.1) that a linear trajectory governs their law of large numbers, namely,
Qj(T) = Qj(0) + T · veff(λj) + O(T 1/2(log T)C),
(1.2)
likely holds, where the effective velocity veff : R →R is an explicit function (see Definition 2.10
below) predicted by Doyon [25, 26] and Spohn [75]. However, (1.2) is insufficient to obtain fine
distributional results (Theorems A, B, and C) for the original Toda lattice. To do the latter, we
must further pinpoint the limiting fluctuations for these quasi-particles.
The joint scaling limit for the fluctuations of all quasi-particles in time is given by the following
theorem (stated informally here), which we view as the core of this paper; Theorems A, B, and C
will follow as consequences of it.
Theorem D (Theorem 2.23 and Corollary E.3). The fluctuations T −1/2 · (Qj(Tτ) −Qj(0) −Tτ ·
veff(λj)) of all quasi-particles converge jointly to an explicit Gaussian process called a dressed L´evy–
Chentsov field (given by Definition 2.21 below), as T tends to ∞.
Fluctuation results as in Theorem D are also known versions of the Navier–Stokes corrections (to
Euler scale ballistic transport) [75, Chapter 15]. We have not seen the full dressed L´evy–Chentsov
field describing these corrections, in the precise form written in Definition 2.21, appear previously.
However, asymptotics for closely related correlation functions were predicted earlier in the physics
literature in [19, 20, 40], including a formula for the limiting diffusivity of a tracer quasi-particle.
We recover the latter from the dressed L´evy–Chentsov field as Proposition 2.24 below.
The term, “dressed L´evy–Chentsov field,” is used since it is a variant of a L´evy–Chentsov field
originating in [50, 15], composed with a nontrivial “dressing operator” (see Definition 2.10 and
Remark 2.22 below). Forms of Theorem D for the hard rods model were proven in [35, 37]. There,
it is the ordinary L´evy–Chentsov field that arises as a scaling limit, as the associated dressing
operator is multiplication by a scalar (essentially since the scattering shift for the hard rods model
4It has also been termed the “flea-gas algorithm” [30] or “semiclassical Bethe equations” [27], since it was predicted
by taking a semi-classical limit of the Bethe equations underlying quantum integrable systems.

is constant). In [37, Section 3.2], it was further explained how a L´evy–Chentsov type convergence
(for quasi-particles initially separated by distances of order T 1/2) can be recast as a fluctuation
result for the hard rods model on diffusive time scales. It is likely possible to show an analog of
that statement for the Toda lattice, as a corollary of Theorem D, but we will not pursue this here.
Certain marginals of the counterpart of Theorem D were also established by Olla–Sasada–Suda
[61] for the box-ball model. They showed that the fluctuations of one box-ball soliton converge to a
Brownian motion, and those of two solitons of the same length (and of distance at most T 1/2 from
each other) converge to shifts of the same Brownian motion. These can be seen to be consistent
with dressed L´evy–Chentsov field fluctuations. Indeed, as a consequence of Theorem D, we deduce
the corresponding results for the Toda lattice as Proposition 2.24 and Corollary 2.25 below (see
also Corollary E.4).

j ). This amounts to showing the following two statements.
(1) Scaling limit for Ξ (Theorem 3.18): Ξ converges, as a process in (Λ, k, t), to a Gaussian
limit X (given by an ordinary L´evy–Chentsov field).
(2) Approximating the (ZQ
j ) through Ξ (Theorem 3.11): The (ZQ
j ) approximate an explicit

Together, the above two statements indicate that the (ZQ
j ) converge to the composition of X
with a certain operator, which will be the dressed L´evy–Chentsov field of Theorem D. Their proofs
occupy most of this work; we remark on them here briefly, leaving further discussion to Section 3.
For the former, observe that Ξ is not a linear statistic of L(0), as it is a function of both its
eigenvalues (Λi) and off-diagonal entries, through the (qi). One aspect of accessing its scaling limit
will involve realizing an independence structure within the sum in (1.6); this uses an approximate
independence between the pairs (Λi, qi+1(0)−qi(0)) and (Λj, qj+1(0)−qj(0)) if |i−j| is large, which
was also used in [1, 2] and can be traced to the localization of eigenvectors of L(0). See the end of
Section 3.4.2 for further commentary, and also for other aspects in identifying the limit of Ξ.
For the second, observe in (1.6) that the integer k is involved in the coefficients there both
through the spatial coordinate αk and the spectral variable Λk. Since it will happen that the latter
is discontinuous in the former, we will generalize (1.6) by replacing every appearance of Λk there
by an arbitrary parameter Λ. We will then guess a solution Z to this new equation, dependent on
two independent parameters (a spectral and a spatial one), obtained by applying an explicit linear
operator (the dressing operator) to Ξ (see (3.14)). Verifying that Z is indeed a solution (Proposition
3.16) requires a concentration bound (Proposition 3.17) to account for the correlation between Z
and the coefficients in this equation. See Section 3.3.2 for further commentary.
Before proceeding, let us make two remarks. First, it is possible that, on a high level, the above
framework may apply to other integrable systems that have microscopically defined quasi-particles
(Qj) satisfying a form of the asymptotic scattering relation (1.1).5 In particular, we expect it should
be correct that, (i) such a relation can be smoothened with negligible error into a form of (1.5), and
the latter can be Taylor expanded into a linear equation analogous to (1.6); (ii) the right side of
this equation converges to a L´evy–Chentsov field; and (iii) the solution to the linear equation from
(i) converges to a dressed L´evy–Chentsov field. If so, proving these statements might still require
model-specific arguments (such as in realizing the independence structure required to show (ii)).
Second, works of Saitoh [67] and Matetski–Quastel–Remenik [53] predict under a certain limit
that the Toda lattice converges to the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) equation; in this limit, thermal
5These are known for the box-ball system (due to works of Takahashi–Satsuma [77], Ferrari–Nguyen–Rolla–Wang
[36], and Croydon–Sasada [18]) and believed for various other integrable models.

equilibrium (when suitably scaled) can be seen to map to white noise. Such a convergence has not
yet been justified, partly since even the well-definedness of the KdV equation under white noise
initial data is quite subtle, proven by Kappeler–Topalov [45] and Quastel–Valk´o [66] on the torus and
by Killip–Murphy–Visan [47] on the line (the latter [47] also used ideas from Anderson localization).
It would be interesting to verify this convergence and see whether the above framework for the Toda
lattice could be adapted to access the long-time asymptotics for the KdV equation.

and Probabilistic Integrable Systems,” held in the spring of 2025 at the Center of Mathematical
Sciences and Applications in Harvard University.
2. Results
2.1. Toda lattice. In this section we recall the Toda lattice on an interval, and its thermal equi-
librium. Throughout, we fix integers N1 ≤N2 and set N = N2 −N1 + 1 (which will prescribe the
interval’s endpoints and length, respectively). We may have N1 = ∞or N2 = −∞; in either case,
N = ∞. We also let Ja, bK = [a, b] ∩Z for any real numbers a ≤b.
The state space of the Toda lattice on the interval JN1, N2K is given by a pair (p(t); q(t)) ∈
RN × RN, where p(t) = (pN1(t), pN1+1(t), . . . , pN2(t)) and q(t) = (qN1(t), qN1+1(t), . . . , qN2(t)) are
N-tuples, both indexed by a real number t ≥0 called the time. Given any initial data (p(0); q(0)) ∈
RN × RN, the joint evolution of (p(t); q(t)) for t ≥0 is prescribed by the system of ordinary
differential equations
∂tqj(t) = pj(t),
and
∂tpj(t) = eqj−1(t)−qj(t) −eqj(t)−qj+1(t),

might interpret this as the dynamics for N points (indexed by JN1, N2K) moving on the real line,
whose locations and momenta at time t ≥0 are given by the (qi(t)) and (pi(t)), respectively.
The system of differential equations (2.1) is equivalent to the Hamiltonian dynamics generated
by the Hamiltonian H : RN × RN →R that is defined, for any p = (pN1, pN1+1, . . . , pN2) ∈RN and
q = (qN1, qN1+1, . . . , qN2) ∈RN, by setting

for example, the proof of [78, Theorem 12.6]).

It will at times be necessary to define the original Toda state space variables (p(t); q(t)) from
the Flaschka variables (a(t); b(t)); it suffices to do this at t = 0, as (p(t); q(t)) is determined from
(p(0); q(0)) and (a(s); b(s))s≥0, by (2.1) and (2.2). We explain how to do this when 0 ∈JN1, N2K
(as will be the case for us). By (2.2), the Flaschka variables a(0) specify the differences between
consecutive entries in q(0), so the former determines the latter up to an overall shift. We fix this
shift by setting q0(0) = 0. Then, (p(0); q(0)) is called the Toda state space initial data associated
with (a(0); b(0)). The evolution (p(t); q(t)) of this initial data under (2.1) is called the Toda state
space dynamics associated with (a(t); b(t)); observe that we may have q0(t) ̸= 0 if t ̸= 0.
Under this notation, we can define the Toda lattice Lax matrix (originally introduced in [39, 52])
as follows.
Definition 2.1 (Lax matrix). Fix t ∈R≥0. Define the Lax matrix L(t) = [Lij(t)]i,j∈JN1,N2K by
Lii(t) = bi,

Also set Lij = 0 for any i, j ∈Z not of the above form.
The use of the Lax matrix is in the following fact, originally due to [39] (see also [59, Section 2]),
indicating that its eigenvalues are conserved.
Lemma 2.2 ([39, 59]). The eigenvalues of L(t) do not depend on t, if N < ∞.
We will study the Toda lattice under random initial data, in which the Flaschka variables are
sampled under a probability measure called thermal equilibrium. In it, the (ai, bi) are mutually
independent, the (ai) are chi random variables, and the (bi) are Gaussian random variables.

AMOL AGGARWAL AND MATTHEW NICOLETTI
Thermal equilibrium µβ,θ;∞is invariant [1, Proposition 2.5] for the Toda lattice on Z (and is
perhaps its most natural invariant measure). We will be interested in analyzing, under thermal
equilibrium, the fluctuations of the Toda lattice’s local charges and associated integrated currents.
To explain them, observe that Lemma 2.2 provides a large family of conserved quantities for
the Toda lattice, given by the eigenvalues of the Lax matrix. However, these are “non-local,” in
the sense that they depend on all of the Flaschka variables (a(t); b(t)), as opposed to only the
(ai(t), bi(t)) for i in some (uniformly) bounded interval. To remedy this, observe that Lemma 2.2
also implies that Tr L(t)m is conserved for any integer m ≥0. Since L(t) is tridiagonal, the diagonal
entries of L(t)m are local quantities, whose total is preserved; they are called local charges and have
associated currents. In what follows, for any matrix M, we let [M]ij denote the (i, j)-entry of M.

2.4. Quasi-particle fluctuations. In this section we first recall the quasi-particles for the Toda
lattice. We then state our fluctuation result (Theorem 2.23) for them, which is what will eventually
enable the proofs of the results from Section 2.3. Throughout this section, we recall the notation
of Section 2.1 (including the Lax matrix L(t) from Definition 2.1), and assume that N1 and N2 are
finite (and thus so is N).
The definition of quasi-particles will make use of localization properties of eigenvectors, so we
begin by setting notation for this.
Definition 2.16. Let u = (u(N1), . . . , u(N2)) ∈RN be a unit vector. For any ζ ∈R≥0, we say
that an index φ ∈JN1, N2K is a ζ-localization center of u if |u(φ)| ≥ζ.
Next, let M be an N ×N real symmetric matrix, with rows and columns indexed by JN1, N2K. If
λ is an eigenvalue of M, we call φ a ζ-localization center for λ if φ is a ζ-localization center of some
unit eigenvector u of M with eigenvalue λ. Further let (u1, u2, . . . , uN) denote an orthonormal
eigenbasis for M. We call a bijection φ : J1, NK →JN1, N2K a ζ-localization center bijection if φ(j)
is a ζ-localization center for uj, for each j = J1, NK. By [1, Lemma 2.7], any symmetric N × N
matrix M admits a (2N)−1-localization center bijection.
Localization center bijections are not always unique. However they, and in fact individual local-
ization centers, are unique up to a small error, if the entries of M are sufficiently random, such as
under thermal equilibrium (see Lemma 3.7 below). Due to this approximate uniqueness, our choice
to take φ to be a bijection is notationally convenient but not essential.


## Page 15


FLUCTUATIONS FOR THE TODA LATTICE
15
To fix the setup, when we work with Toda lattice on a finite interval JN1, N2K, we will typically
impose the following assumption.
Assumption 2.17. As in Definition 2.7, fix real numbers β, θ > 0, and assume that α ̸= 0. For
each real number t ≥0, let L(t) = [Lij(t)]i,j∈JN1,N2K denote the Lax matrix for the Toda lattice
(a(t); b(t)) on JN1, N2K (as in Definition 2.1). Denote the eigenvalues of L(t) by λ1 ≥λ2 ≥· · · ≥λN,
which do not depend on t by Lemma 2.2. At t = 0, abbreviate L = L(0) and (a; b) = (a(0); b(0)).
Assume that the initial data (a(0); b(0)) is sampled under the thermal equilibrium µβ,θ;N1,N2 from
Definition 2.3. Let (p(s); q(s)), over s ∈R≥0, denote the Toda state space dynamics associated
with (a(t); b(t)). Further let T ≥1 and ζ ≥0 be real numbers, and suppose that

Let us briefly comment on Assumption 2.17. First, we set the interval JN1, N2K to have its length
N be much larger than the time T, so that the behavior of the Toda lattice near its endpoints (which
do not exist on Z) does not affect its bulk. While in (2.28) we made the specific choice N = ⌊T 100⌋,
the parameter T could have been taken to grow with N as quickly as N(log N)−C for C sufficiently
large. However, we do not pursue this here, as the setting of Assumption 2.17 will suffice to prove
the results stated in Section 2.3. Second, the precise choice of ζ in (2.28) is not essential; we only
require it to decay faster than a polynomial, but slower than stretched exponentially, in N.
Now, following [1, Equation (2.9)], we can define the quasi-particles mentioned in Section 1.2.
Definition 2.18 (Quasi-particles). Adopt Assumption 2.17. For each s ∈R, let uj(s) ∈RN denote
a unit eigenvector of L(s) with eigenvalue λj. Under the orthonormal basis (u1(s), u2(s), . . . , uN(s))
of L(s), let φs : J1, NK →JN1, N2K be a ζ-localization center bijection for L(s), and define the quasi-
particle

which we view as the eigenvalue with localization center i at time t. Also abbreviate Λi = Λi(0).
While the above defines quasi-particles for the Toda lattice on the long but finite interval
JN1, N2K, it is also possible to define them directly on the infinite line Z, without changing any
of the asymptotics results to be stated in this section; this is done in Appendix E below. However,
for the purposes of proving the results from Section 2.3, it will in fact be more convenient to analyze
the scaling limits of these quasi-particles on the interval JN1, N2K above (which will in any case be
used to prove their infinite volume counterparts in Appendix E, through a limiting procedure).
As mentioned in (1.2), it was shown in [2] that (for θ ≤θ0(β) sufficiently small) the quasi-
particles Qj(t) evolve approximately linearly with velocity veff(λj) (as in Definition 2.10), namely,
Qi(t)−Qi(0) = tveff(λi)+O(T 1/2+o(1)). The results from Section 2.3 will be eventual consequences
of an exact computation of the scaling limit for the fluctuations of the quasi-particles.
In order to state this result (that gives the precise form of Theorem D), we must define the limiting

the dressing operator (1−θTϱβ)−1 act on the second argument λ of f. Since (1−θTϱβ)−1 : H →H
is bounded and f ∈L2(dr ⊗ϱdλ), we have (1 −θTϱβ)−1f ∈L2(dr ⊗ϱdλ).

Remark 2.22 (X as a L´evy–Chentsov field). The Gaussian process X(s) = Wdr(ψΛ,κ,τ) from (2.35)
is an example of what is known in the literature as a L´evy–Chentsov field [50, 15, 35]. Recall from
Section 1.3 that it will govern the fluctuations of the field Ξ from (1.7). Indeed, the form (2.35) of
X (recalling ψ from (2.32)) looks analogous to that of Ξ in (1.7), upon replacing χ(x) there with
1x>0; the qi(0) there with αi; and the sum over i there with an integral against Wdr.
Let us briefly comment on the structure of X. Define the noise ωΛ = 2 log |Λ −λ| · Wdr, which
is a random distribution acting on functions f ∈L2(dr ⊗ϱdλ) by
ωΛ(f) = Wdr(2f log |Λ −λ|).
For any Λ ∈R, using the noise ωΛ one can define a function η : R3 →R, sometimes known [35,
Section 2] as a L´evy-Chentsov field, with respect to ωΛ as follows. First, for any q, Λ, τ ∈R, define
the functions L+,Λ(q, τ), L−,Λ(q, τ) : R2 →R by
L+,Λ(q, τ)(r, λ) := 1{αr > q, αr + τveff(λ) < q + τveff(Λ)};
L−,Λ(q, τ)(r, λ) := 1{αr < q, αr + τveff(λ) > q + τveff(Λ)}.
One may view these functions as indexing lines crossing the space-time segment connecting (q, 0)
to (q + τveff(Λ), τ), from right to left and from left to right, respectively. Then, let
η(Λ, q, τ) := ωΛ(L+,Λ(q, τ)) −ωΛ(L−,Λ(q, τ)).

With this definition, the field X(Λ, κ, τ) = Wdr(ψΛ,κ,τ) may be identified with η via
{X(Λ, κ, τ)}(Λ,κ,τ)∈R3 d= {η(Λ, ακ, τ)}(Λ,κ,τ)∈R3,
which is a variant of the L´evy–Chentsov field considered in [35, Section 2] (with a different choice
of the Hilbert space H and of the covariance for the white noise W).
Since Z is a dressing of X, we refer to Z as a dressed L´evy–Chentsov field.
The following theorem, proven in Section 3.2, states that this dressed L´evy–Chentsov field governs
the fluctuations for quasi-particles in the Toda lattice. The analog of Theorem 2.23 can also be
shown for the Toda lattice on the infinite line Z; see Corollary E.3 below.

Observe that Theorem 2.23 simultaneously couples every quasi-particle (in an order T neighbor-
hood of the origin) to the dressed L´evy–Chentsov field. Thus, it provides the limiting correlations
between quasi-particles that are both initially very distant (say, of order T apart) and closer (related
to Navier–Stokes corrections); Corollary 2.25 below provides a consequence for closer ones.

We conclude this section with the following result essentially indicating that the fluctuations of
quasi-particles, with nearly equal spectral parameters, are asymptotically completely correlated, as
long as their initial separation is o(T). It is a quick consequence of Theorem 2.23, together with
the continuity of the dressed L´evy–Chentsov field Z, and it will be shown in Section 10.2. This
result can also be shown for the Toda lattice on the infinite line Z; see Corollary E.4 below.

Let N ≥1 be an integer and EN be an event depending on N (and possibly other parameters).
We say that EN holds with overwhelming probability, or is overwhelmingly probable, if there exists
a constant c > 0 such that P[E∁
N] ≤c−1e−c(log N)2. Here, c is an absolute constant, meaning that it
is independent of N (and all other parameters involved in the definition of EN, except for possibly

We will often study fluctuations of the form X −E[X], for a random variable given by a fairly
intricate sum. So, to ease notation, we may occasionally abbreviate X −E[X] = X −E[· · · ] (where
it should be understood that E[· · · ] = E[X] is chosen to ensure that X −E[· · · ] has mean 0).
At the beginning of each (sub)section, we will recall the notation that will be used (without
comment) throughout the (sub)section.
3. Proof of quasi-particle fluctuations
As we will see in Sections 11 and 12 below, our results in Section 2.3 will follow from the quasi-
particle fluctuations given by Theorem 2.23 (together with known results approximating currents
and local charges through quasi-particles). So, in this section, we establish the latter, conditional
on various intermediate results that will be proven later in this paper. Throughout this section, we
recall the notation from Assumption 2.17 and Definition 2.18.

q = ακ + τveff(Λk), we must confirm that (Λk, q, τ) ∈[−log N, log N] × [−(log N)3, (log N)3] ×
[0, log N] holds, for all (k, t) ∈J−T log N, T log NK × [0, log N], with overwhelming probability.
Fix such a pair (k, t). By Lemma 3.6, the event BNDL(0)(log N) from Definition 3.6 holds with
overwhelming probability, so we may restrict to it.
Then |Λk| ≤log N, so the second part of
Lemma 3.2 gives |veff(Λk)| ≤C log N. Hence, |q| ≤(|α|κ + Cτ) log N ≤(log N)3, so (Λk, q, τ) ∈
[−log N, log N] × [−(log N)3, (log N)3] × [0, log N], which shows the theorem.
□

here is made more intricate by the fact that Z depends on all eigenvalues of L(0) (as opposed to
on only a single one).

While we will not establish Theorem 3.24 here, let us briefly comment on its proof. We restrict
the discussion below to (Ξ, Ξ1), as similar reasoning will apply to (Ξ[m], Ξ[m]
1
).
1. First, we show that the processes Ξ1 and Ξ−Ξ1 can be approximated by sums of independent
random variables; see Proposition 5.4. To heuristically see this for the difference Ξ−Ξ1, recall that
Ξ and Ξ1 differ in that the arguments of χ involve qi(0) in the definition (3.13) of Ξ but involve αi
in that (3.35) of Ξ1. Thus, Taylor expanding Ξ−Ξ1 to first order yields a linear combination of the
(qi(0) −αi), with random coefficients (dependent on the eigenvalues (Λi)) that should average to
deterministic ones. Since the (qi −qi−1) are independent under thermal equilibrium (by (2.2) and
Definition 2.6), this linear combination is expressible as a sum of independent random variables.
For Ξ1, the underlying heuristic is different and will be based on the notion that the eigenvalues
Λi of L(0) are approximately local; this means that they essentially only depend on the entries of
L(0) near the (i, i) one (see Lemma 4.10 for a more precise statement). By the independence of the
entries in L(0), this indicates that Λi and Λj are almost independent, unless |i −j| is small. Hence,
if one subdivides the sum over i in the definition (3.35) of Ξ1 into small intervals (say, of length
T c), the resulting summands should nearly be independent, providing the approximation for Ξ1 as
a sum of independent random variables.
2. Second, we identify the limiting covariances of certain linear statistics of L(0), with effective
rates of convergence; see Proposition 6.1. For polynomial test functions, it is known [57] that these
covariances tend to explicit limits, but without a rate of convergence. In our context, we must
allow the test functions to involve the smoothed logarithm l, and we must ensure that errors are
polynomial in T (to offset the factors of (log N)C that frequently arise in errors, such as in Lemma
3.9). This is done through a multi-scale argument, using the above-mentioned approximate locality
of eigenvalues of L(0) and estimates from polynomial approximation theory.
3. Third, we use the above two statements to couple (Ξ1, Ξ−Ξ1) to a Gaussian process. When re-
stricting the parameters (Λ, κ, τ) to a mesh of spacing T −c, this follows from known high-dimensional
Gaussian approximation results [4] (see also [83]). To extend these parameters to their full domains,
we require a H¨older-type continuity estimate of Ξ; this is shown as Lemma 7.2, using concentration
estimates for functionals of random matrices.
3.5. Outline. The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 4 we collect pre-
liminary identities and estimates that will be used throughout. In Section 5 we show how certain


## Page 30


30
AMOL AGGARWAL AND MATTHEW NICOLETTI
functionals (involving both eigenvalues and localization centers) of the random Lax matrix L(0)
can be approximated by sums of independent variables. In Section 6 we show effective convergence
results for the limiting covariances of linear statistics of L(0). In Section 7 we show continuity
bounds on Ξ and Ξ[m], which we use to establish the Gaussian coupling result Theorem 3.24 in
Section 8. In Section 9 we show Proposition 3.17, which we use in Section 10 to prove Theorem
3.11. In Section 11 we apply these results to compute fluctuations of integrated currents, proving
Theorem 2.12. Finally, Section 12 computes fluctuations of local charges and uses them to analyze
associated two-point functions, proving Corollary 2.15. The proofs of several auxiliary results are
deferred to the appendices.
4. Miscellaneous preliminaries
In this section we collect some preliminary facts that will be used throughout the paper. The
statements here are either known or consequences of known results, so we defer their proofs (when
we do not have a reference for them) to the Appendices.

4.3. Approximate locality of eigenvalues. In this section we state a result indicating that the
eigenvalues of a Lax matrix L (Definition 2.1), sampled under thermal equilibrium (Definition 2.3),
are “approximately local,” in that they essentially only depend on a few entries of the matrix. To
make this precise, the following assumption begins with a random Lax matrix L and “resamples”
entries indexed by some subset D, to form a new matrix ˜L.
Assumption 4.9. Adopt Assumption 2.17. Let ˜L = [˜Lij] be another random, tridiagonal sym-
metric matrix, with rows and columns indexed by JN1, N2K, that is obtained from L in one of the
following three ways. Below, we sample ˜a = (˜ai)i∈JN1,N2−1K and ˜b = (˜bi)i∈JN1,N2K under thermal
equilibrium (2.6), independently of L. Moreover, we let D ⊆JN1, N2K denote a subset of indices.
(1) For each i /∈D, we set ˜Li,i+1 = ˜Li+1,i = Li,i+1 = Li+1,i = ai and ˜Lii = Lii = bi. For each
i ∈D, we set ˜Li+1,i = ˜Li,i+1 = ˜ai and ˜Lii = ˜bi.
(2) For each i, j /∈D, we set Lij = ˜Lij. Otherwise, we set ˜Lij = 0.
(3) Fix a real number t ∈[0, T(log N)10]. Let ˜L have the same law as L(t), and be coupled
with L such that the following holds. For D = [N1, N1 + T 2] ∪[N2 −T 2, N2], we have with
overwhelming probability that |Lij −˜Lij| ≤e−T 2/5 for all i, j /∈D. The existence of such
an ˜L follows from [1, Proposition 2.5].
The following lemma indicates that the eigenvalues and their associated localization centers (if
they are not too close to D) of L are approximately equal to those of ˜L. Its first and second parts
follow from [1, Corollary 5.5 ] and [1, Corollary 5.6], respectively, with the δ there equal to either
0 (in the first two cases of Assumption 4.9) or e−T 2/5 < e−(log N)3 (in the third case).
Lemma 4.10 ([1, Corollaries 5.5 and 5.6]). Adopt Assumption 4.9, and recall Definition 2.18.
There exists a constant c > 0 such that the following holds with overwhelming probability.
(1) For each i ∈JN1, N2K with dist(i, D) > (log N)3, there is an eigenvalue ˜λ ∈eig ˜L such that
|Λi −˜λ| ≤e−c(log N)3,
and i is an N −1ζ-localization center for ˜λ.
(2) For each i ∈JN1, N2K with dist(i, D) > (log N)3, there is a unique ψ(i) ∈JN1, N2K such
that
|Λψ(i) −˜Λi| ≤e−c(log N)3.
Moreover, this ψ(i) satisfies |ψ(i) −i| ≤(log N)2.

6. Effective convergence of linear statistics
In this section we establish an effective estimate, given by Proposition 6.2, for the limiting
variance of linear statistics of Lax matrices under from thermal equilibrium.
Throughout this
section, we let L = [Lij] denote an N × N Lax matrix (Definition 2.1), with rows and columns
indexed by i, j ∈J1, NK, sampled from thermal equilibrium (Definition 2.3).
Throughout this
section, neither Assumption 2.17 nor the localization centers of Definition 2.16 will be used; thus,
without loss of generality we have shifted set JN1, N2K indexing the rows and columns of L to
J1, NK. Further define the stretch random variables Xi as in Definition 5.2, where the qi there are
determined from the ai = Li,i+1 here by (2.2). Also let eig L = (λ1, λ2, . . . , λN). Throughout, we
further recall the notation from Definitions 2.10 and 4.6, which will be often used. To ease notation,
throughout the section we fix the constant
γ = 3/5.

The proof essentially proceeds by viewing the N × N Lax matrix LN as consisting of N −1N
submatrices of size N × N, each with the same law as L. We will then show that two different such
N × N Lax submatrices are approximately independent. This will enable us to approximate the
variance of a linear statistic for LN by N −1N multiplied by the corresponding variance for L.
Lemma 6.5 below makes precise the above notion of “approximate independence,” by estimating
the covariances of distant resolvent entries of LN. Before stating it, we state a quick bound on the
difference between covariances of random variables that are likely approximately equal.

6.4. Proof of Proposition 6.1. In this section we prove Proposition 6.1; we adopt the notation
of that proposition throughout.
We will proceed by first replacing the function ϕ with a proxy ψ, which is analytic in a strip of
the complex plane around the real line. Due to its analyticity, ψ will be closely approximable by
a polynomial p. After verifying that p satisfies Assumption 6.2, we will apply Corollary 6.7 to p,
where the limiting variance there has the explicit form given by Lemma 4.7. Since p ≈ψ ≈ϕ, we
will then deduce that the same formulas hold with p replaced by ϕ, confirming the proposition.

7. Continuity of prelimit processes
Throughout this section, we adopt Assumptions 2.17 and 5.1, and we recall the notation in
Definitions 3.8, 3.10, 3.20, 3.21, and 5.2. Moreover, we assume throughout (even when not explicitly
stated) that θ < θ0(β) is sufficiently small, so that all of the results from Sections 3, 4, and 5 apply.
7.1. Continuity bounds. In this section we prove the below continuity bound for the functional
Ξ from Definition 3.10.
Proposition 7.1 (Continuity of Ξ). Let S ∈[M, T] be a real number.
There exist constants
c > 0 and C > 1 such that the following holds with overwhelming probability. For any integers
k1, k2 ∈J−T 3/2, T 3/2K with |k1 −k2| ≤S; real numbers t1, t2 ∈[0, T log N] with |t1 −t2| ≤S; and
real numbers Λ1, Λ2 ∈[−log N, log N] with |Λ1 −Λ2| ≤ST −1, we have
|Ξ(Λ1, k1, t1) −Ξ(Λ2, k2, t2)| ≤(S1/4T −1/4 + T −c)(log N)C.

8. Coupling with a Gaussian process
In this section we prove Theorem 3.24. Throughout this section, we adopt Assumptions 2.17 and
we recall the notation from (2.19) and Definitions 2.11, 2.19, 2.21, 3.8, 3.10, 3.21, 3.22, and 4.6.
Moreover, we assume throughout (even when not explicitly stated) that θ < θ0(β) is sufficiently
small (so that all of the results from Sections 3, 4, 5, and 7 apply).
8.1. Comparing prelimiting and limiting variances. To motivate the following preliminary
lemmas, recall the Gaussian process Wdr (Definition 2.19), whose covariance structure of Wdr is
related to the bilinear form C (Definition 4.6). Specifically, recall that Wdr is a Gaussian process
indexed by functions f ∈L2(dr⊗ϱdλ) (from (2.19)). In what follows, for any f ∈L2(dr⊗ϱdλ) and
r ∈R, define fr ∈H by setting fr(λ) := f(r, λ) for each λ ∈R; we also set µf : R →R by setting
µf(r) = µfr (Definition 4.6) for each r ∈R. The Gaussian process Wdr has the property that for
any two functions f, g ∈L2(dr ⊗ϱdλ), the pair (Wdr(f −µfς0), Wdr(g −µgς0)) is (by (4.7)) jointly
Gaussian with covariance
(8.1)
CovWdr(f −µfς0, g −µgς0) =

8.2. Gaussian coupling lemmas. In this section we state two lemmas that will be used in the
proof of Theorem 3.24. The first provides, in the special case we need, a multi-dimensional version
of the Kolm´os–Major–Tusn´ady (KMT) coupling. The second states that multivariate Gaussian
random variables, with approximately equal covariance matrices, can be coupled to likely be close
to each other.
Below, for any integer d ≥0 and d-dimensional random variable v = (v1, v2, . . . , vd), the covari-
ance matrix of v is the d × d matrix, whose (i, j)-entry is equal to Cov(vi, vj) for each i, j ∈J1, dK.
Lemma 8.3 ([4, Equation (1.1)]). There exist constants c > 0 and C > 1 such that the following
holds. Let n, d ≥1 be integers; B > 0 be a real number; and Y1, . . . , Yn ∈Rd be mutually in-
dependent, centered (d-dimensional) random variables, such that max1≤i≤n ∥Yi∥2 ≤B. Then, for
any real number δ > 0, there exists a coupling between (Y1, . . . , Yn) and a family (Z1, . . . , Zn) of
mutually independent, centered (d-dimensional) Gaussian random variables, such that Yi and Zi

9. Concentration estimate for sums of Z
In this section we establish Proposition 3.17, which provides a concentration estimate for linear
combinations of the function Z from (3.14).
Throughout this section, we adopt Assumption 2.17 and recall the notation from Definitions
2.18 and 3.10. Moreover, we assume throughout (even when not explicitly stated) that θ < θ0(β)
is sufficiently small (so that all of the results from the previous sections apply).
9.1. Bounds on Z. In this section we show several estimates for Z(Λ, Q) = Z(Λ, Q, t) defined by
(3.14) (below we also recall the process Z from Definition 2.21).
Lemma 9.1. There exists a constant C > 1 such that the following holds with overwhelming
probability. For any real number Λ ∈R, we have

Remark 10.3. The random variable τ 7→Z(Λ, q + τveff(Λ), τ) represents the scaling limit of
the fluctuations of a tracer quasi-particle with spectral parameter Λ and macroscopic position
q, at time τ.
There does not appear to be a unique microscopic definition of such a “tracer
quasi-particle” in the literature, although there are multiple reasonable notions of it; let us briefly
discuss two here (recalling Definition 2.18 below). One way to define a tracer quasi-particle, with
spectral parameter Λ and initial position qk(0) ≈αk (for some index k), is to set it equal to Qk(t),
conditional on the event that {Λk ≈Λ}. A second way is to impose that its trajectory QΛ(t)

AMOL AGGARWAL AND MATTHEW NICOLETTI
satisfies the asymptotic scattering relation describing quasi-particles, but with a fixed value of its
spectral parameter; specifically, it can be set to an approximate solution of
(10.20)
QΛ(t) ≈QΛ(0) + Λt + 2 sgn(α)

!
.
In the scaling limit, one should have
QΛ(t) := QΛ(0) + tveff(Λ) + T 1/2 · Z(Λ, q, τ).
(10.21)
Indeed, for Λ = Λk we have by Theorem 2.23 that these describe the fluctuations of a quasi-
particle with spectral parameter Λk, which is in heuristic alignment with the first description above
(especially since the coupling in Theorem 2.23 holds with overwhelming probability, suggesting
that it should persist under conditioning on events such as {Λk ≈Λ}). Moreover, (10.21) can be
shown (though we will not pursue this in detail here) to be an approximate solution to (10.20), in
alignment with the second description above.
11. Fluctuations of integrated currents

the notation from Definitions 2.4, 2.5, 2.19, 2.21, 2.18, and 3.8.

11.3. Proof of Theorem 2.12. In this section we prove Theorem 2.12. Using Theorem 11.7, this
will essentially follow from the coupling between the Toda lattices on JN1, N2K and Z, provided by
Lemma 11.3.
Proof of Theorem 2.12. First, we set some notation. Denote a(t) = (ai(t))i∈Z and b(t) = (bi(t))i∈Z,
and recall that (a(0); b(0)) is sampled under thermal equilibrium µβ,θ;∞from (2.6). Let T ≥1
be a real number; set N = ⌊T 100⌋; and let N1 ≤N2 be integers satisfying Assumption 2.17.
Let (˜a(t); ˜b(t)) denote the Toda lattice on JN1, N2K, with initial data (˜a(0); ˜b(0)) sampled under
thermal equilibrium µβ,θ;N1,N2, let (˜p(t); ˜q(t)) denote the associated Toda state space dynamics.

12. Fluctuations of local charges
In this section we prove Corollary 2.15. To show Corollary 2.15, we will first prove Theorem
12.1 below, which proves a joint scaling limit for certain linear functionals of local charges at fixed
times. Throughout we assume θ < θ0(β) is sufficiently small and we adopt Assumption 2.17 when
applicable.

C.5. Proof of Lemma 3.19. We will show the two statements (3.31) and (3.32) of Lemma 3.19
separately.
Proof of (3.31). This follows immediately from Item 3 of Lemma 4.17, except for the uniformity in
t. The overwhelmingly probable extension to all t ∈[0, T log N] follows from a union bound over a
lattice mesh, together with a continuity estimate. We omit further details on this argument, as it
is entirely analogous to the proofs of Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4.
□

Appendix D. Variance of linear statistic
Below we will deal with a random collection of numbers a = (a1, . . . , aN−1) and b = (b1, . . . , bN),
where N > 0 is an integer. In all cases, will denote by L = L(a, b) the symmetric tridiagonal N ×N
matrix with Li,i+1 = Li+1,i = ai for i ∈J1, N −1K, and Lii = bi, for i = J1, NK (and Lij = 0 for all
(i, j) not of the above form). In all that follows, will also let V (x) denote a polynomial potential
function, which we always assume has an even degree d ≥2, and positive leading coefficient.
Throughout this appendix, θ > 0 will be a real number.

minimize a certain variational problem, and recall certain facts about them (from the prior works
[43, 32]) or prove several for them.

Appendix E. Quasi-particles in infinite volume
Recall that quasi-particles were defined in Section 2.4 (see Definition 2.17) for the Toda lattice
on a long, but finite, interval JN1, N2K. In this section we first define these quasi-particles directly
on the infinite line Z (the analog of this for the box-ball system was done in [36]). We then use
Theorem 2.23 to deduce their fluctuation scaling limit. Throughout, we fix real numbers β, θ > 0.
E.1. Quasi-particles on Z. Let (a(t); b(t)) denote the Flaschka variables of the Toda lattice on Z,
initialized under thermal equilibrium µβ,θ;∞(Definition 2.3), where a(t) = (ai(t)) and b(t) = (bi(t)).
Also, let (p(t); q(t)) denote the associated state space dynamics (recall Section 2.1), and let L(t) =
[Lij(t)] denote the associated Lax matrix as in (2.5) (with the (N1, N2) there equal to (−∞, ∞)
here), which is an operator on the Hilbert space ℓ2(Z) = {(vi)i∈Z ∈RZ : P
i∈Z v2
i < ∞}. By [78,
Remark 1.10], almost surely each eigenvalue of L(t) has multiplicity one, for all t ≥0. Let eig L(t)

As indicated by the following definition, the Qλ(t) from (E.1) will be the quasi-particles for the

Observe that, while the quasi-particles for the Toda lattice on a finite interval (see (2.29)) are
permutations of the original Toda particle locations (qi(t)), the Qλ(t) are sums of these locations,
weighted by the eigenvector entries.
We will see that these entries are localized on only a few
coordinates, so the Qλ(t) will closely approximate the (qi(t)) (see (E.10) below).

E.1. For any i ∈Z, set Λi = Φ(i), and define the quasi-particle Qi(t) = QΛi(t) for any t ∈R≥0.

E.2. Approximations by finite Lax matrices and quasi-particles. In this section we show
Lemma E.1 and Corollary E.3, by approximating the Lax operator L(t) by finite-dimensional ones.

Proof of Lemma E.5 (Outline). We only outline the proof of this lemma, since it largely follows
from results in [1, Section 5]. It suffices to show it for any integer N ′ ∈J2N, 4NK, as then the
lemma would follow by induction on ⌊log2(N ′/N)⌋. Its first part follows from Lemma 4.10, applied
with the (L; ˜L; D) there equal to (LN′(0); LN(0); J−N ′, N ′K \ J−N, NK) here. In what follows, we
restrict to the event E = SEPLN(0)(e−c0(log N)2/4)∩SEPLN′(0)(e−c0(log N)2/4) (as we may, by Lemma
4.5). Then, the third part of the lemma follows from the first.
It remains to verify the second part of the lemma. We will only do so for a fixed choice of t ≥0,
as then the proof that it holds for all t simultaneously follows from a union bound over a lattice
mesh, with a continuity estimate. We omit further details on the latter, as it is entirely analogous
to the proofs of Lemmas 7.3 and 7.4 (using the fact that |∂tui;N(k; t)| ≤CN 2 with probability at
least 1 −c−1e−c(log N)2, by [1, Equation (5.4)] and Lemma 3.6).

z ∈C, set z = λi;N + iη where η = e−(log N)5/2 and denote the resolvents GN(z) = (LN(t) −z)−1,
GN′(z) = (LN′(t) −z)−1, and G(z) = (M −z)−1. Then, by [1, Lemma 5.4] (with Lemma 3.6), we

of η = e−(log N)5/2, and our restriction to E. This, with (E.15), yields |ui;N(k; t)2 −ui′;N ′(k; t)2| ≤
e−(log N)2 if |k| ≤4N/5. Since |φ| ≤N/2 and φ is an e−150(log N)3/2-localization center for λi;N
with respect to LN(t), this in particular implies that ui′;N ′(φ; t)2 ≥ui;N(φ; t)2 −e−(log N)2 ≥
e−150(log N′)3/2, so φ is an e−150(log N′)3/2-localization center for λi′;N ′ with respect to LN(t).
It remains to show that |ui;N(k; t)2 −ui′;N ′(k; t)2| ≤e−c(log N)2 for |k| > 4N/5. In this case,
|k −φ| > N/4 > T(log N)2. Hence, by [1, Equation (5.1)], we have with probability at least 1 −
c−1e−c(log N)2 that ui;N(k; t)2 ≤e−(log N)2 and ui′;N ′(k; t)2 ≤e−(log N)2, establishing the lemma.
